i-9DnGFSTnRcFbFiuJKehqHP	Madam President, I would like Today, we are once again debating measures following Russia's invasion of Ukraine. It is a war that has been going on for more than two months now. It is a war whose cruelty does not cease to touch us, hour by hour, day after day. The government's strategy in this very serious situation is clear. We will support Ukraine, push for sanctions against Russia and strengthen Sweden. In the additional amending budget that we are debating today, the focus is on the latter – strengthening Sweden and making it easier for ordinary people who have been hit hard by increased fuel prices when Russia screwed its cranes with energy to Europe. If Parliament says yes later today, the tax on diesel and petrol will be reduced from 1 May to the EU minimum for five months. The reduction of the energy tax by SEK 1 and 5 øre means that the price at pump is reduced by SEK 1 and 31 øre, at an estimated cost of SEK 3.9 billion. Together with the permanent reduction in the tax decided by Parliament last autumn, the tax reductions have helped to reduce the price by l kroner and 80 öre. The supplementary amending budget also contains proposals to authorise the donation of surplus doses of covid-19 vaccine. Covid-19 is no longer classified as a generally dangerous and socially dangerous disease, but the need for vaccines to avoid serious disease remains great around the world. Russia's invasion of Ukraine has greatly affected the world market price of oil and gas. We also see an increase in the prices of food, for example. People around Europe are directly affected in their everyday lives, and Sweden is no exception. No government can credibly promise to compensate for these price increases in full, but to mitigate the impact on households and businesses, the government has presented a wide range of measures. Our entrance has been to get in place as quickly as possible measures that can actually become a reality. The package of measures includes, among other things, the specific tax reduction that we are deciding on today. It is an important and powerful measure that provides a relief for households at a time of very volatile fuel prices. But we also see the importance of more accurate distribution policies. We therefore propose, for example, an increase in the housing allowance for families with children. But we are now in a new situation. International organisations such as the OECD and the IMF warn against giving too general support. It risks driving inflation and eroding public finances, a situation that would only further harm the households and businesses we are trying to support. In this situation, measures supporting households with the weakest economies are advocated, those who, even before the price increases, twisted every penny to make the money last for the necessary whole month. It is an analysis that is important to have with you in the further work. Madam President, I would like The uncertain economic situation also makes it important to safeguard the orderly and coherent budgetary process. These are the principles which enabled us to face both the Corona and the new security policy situation with a strong economy. When we entered the pandemic, the public debt was the lowest since 1977. In crisis, of course, that position of strength should be used. But even in times of crisis, responsibility for taxpayers' money is important. For example, the importance of good preparation needs to be weighed against the need to get action on the ground quickly. That is precisely why there should be special reasons for the government to present an additional amending budget by the end of the day. If there are no specific reasons, the issues should be dealt with within the framework of regular budgetary processes. Madam President, I would like Today we are also dealing with the proposals for announcements by the majority of committees. They are about supporting agriculture to ensure Swedish food readiness and increased support to civil society for their work on the reception of refugees from Ukraine. Swedish agriculture has been severely affected by increased costs for several inputs in the wake of Russia's invasion of Ukraine. Securing Swedish food production is important not least for Swedish food readiness in the event of a crisis. The government has therefore now, as in previous crises such as drought, taken action. Earlier this spring, a first package of measures of SEK 1.1 billion was presented, and last Monday the government, together with the Center Party, presented an additional package of measures containing aid for the livestock sector, increased Norrland support, support for professional fishing and retroactive refund of diesel tax. In total, it is about SEK 3.2 billion. Ensuring sustainable food production in Sweden and the EU requires a common agricultural policy that is stable and does not undermine competition in the market. It would be unfortunate if the Member States of the European Union were to increasingly request derogations from and depart from the EU rules that we have jointly agreed. We are therefore critical of the proposal by the majority of committees to press for derogations from EU state aid rules in a number of areas without a deeper analysis. Such a choice can have very negative consequences for Swedish agriculture. Therefore, such choices need to be thoroughly analysed. We have therefore reserved ourselves against the announcement. During the process, we have also raised the eyebrows of the order that parties in the committee have merged their wish lists on support for agriculture rather than agreeing on a reasonable priority. With regard to the proposal for the announcement of increased funding for civil society, we naturally share the view that civil society and religious communities are doing an important job in order for the reception and integration to work well. In order to support civil society's efforts, the government has therefore proposed SEK 100 million in the amending budget to civil society organisations specifically for efforts for protection from the war in Ukraine. That is why we believe it is premature that the parliamentary majority already wants to establish that there are specific reasons for an additional amending budget with additional support for civil society. But the government will continue to follow developments and, of course, is prepared to take further action if necessary. Madam President, I would like I would therefore like to support the Government's draft supplementary amending budget, that is to say, in paragraph 1 of the report, and also to endorse all the reservations expressed by the Social Democrats, that is to say, in paragraphs 2 and 3.
i-PWpwjeDgC3wVwdbviNa4Ry	Madam President, I would like As you know, there is a terrible war going on in Ukraine. It is illegal and terrible in every way possible, and innocent civilians are affected and killed. Of course, we in this House spend much of our time thinking about what we and Sweden can do to put an end to the war as soon as possible. That is why the Green Party has proposed an immediate halt to all imports of Russian energy, whether gas, oil, uranium or biofuels. It should of course be stopped at EU level if possible, but Sweden should also be able to show that we are a pioneer country and do it unilaterally. Beyond the issue of an end to the war, of course, there is also the question of the effects here in Sweden on households and how we can support that we all get through the situation. These two questions should not work against each other and stand against each other. We cannot, with one hand, try to stop the war and, with the other, act in this House in a way that fattens Putin's war fund. Fortunately, there are those who are not just sitting around and enjoying this, but who are experts and researchers and who have a competence that we in this House lack in terms of how to proceed. I was going to quote some of them. It is Johan Gars, environmental economist at the Beijer Institute, Daniel Spiro, economist at Uppsala University and Henrik Wachtmeister, researcher in global energy systems at Uppsala University. These three have written a debate article in Dagens Nyheter where they write about how Sweden can act and how Europe should act so as not to fatten Putin's war fund in this terrible situation. They have written a research study on how much Russia benefits from the fact that EU countries are now moving on a broad front, almost all countries, with reduced taxes on fuel. They write on DN Debate: a zero-sum game that involves large extra revenues for Russia – possibly up to 400 million SEK per day in the first month. The effect then decreases a little, but it still means SEK 40 billion on an annual basis. And this is in addition to the SEK 6 billion a day for which the EU is already buying Russian energy today. Just to take this 400 million, which then gets a little smaller per day, and put them in context of what it means for Putin: that means the equivalent of 1 000 professional soldiers for a year. So what Putin benefits in a single day by European countries lowering the tax on fuels, he can spend 1 000 professional soldiers for a year. That's the actual numbers. What I want to show with the quote and the debate article is that what seven out of eight parties in this House are doing right now is, with one hand, wanting to put an end to the war but with the other, acting in a way that fattens Putin's war fund. That is exactly what we should certainly not do if we want to destroy Russia's ability to continue its war of aggression in Ukraine as soon as possible. This is happening at the same time as we also live in a climate emergency. What happens when you lower the tax on fossil fuels is that you contribute to maintaining high demand. That is what fattens Putin war fund, but it is also what is currently causing us to see that several countries around the world are now opening up new oil fields. It is about the way we act in this House. It is also about many other parliaments, but it is not least what seven out of eight parties are doing in this House that is leading us now to open new oil fields – oil fields that might not have been opened otherwise. Several new oil fields around the world have been announced because of this sustained demand for fossil fuels. This action is dangerous both for the war and for the climate. The strange thing is that it happens in a situation where we actually have alternatives. It is possible to act in a way to stop the war and to support our households and businesses through the crisis. You can do as the Green Party has done: we have put forward major crisis aid and large aid packages that are about getting out of the situation of fossil dependence rather than fattening it. It is, for example, a question of speeding up electrification in the transport sector. We want to give extra support to those who live in rural areas and buy an electric car. If you also scrap your fossil car you get additional support; you can get up to 90 000 SEK if you scrap a fossil car and buy an electric car. We need to think about energy efficiency. There is a historylessness here, because in previous crises there has been number one. Then you've said, "Saming to work!" Act to reduce your energy needs before we support for more energy use! During the oil crisis and in previous war situations, Swedish policy has acted to achieve more energy efficiency. We need to focus more on that. We can support by lowering taxes on other things. For example, we can reduce taxes widely for those living in sparsely populated areas. The researchers I'm talking about mention in their article that the corresponding amount of money directly into the wallet for ordinary people gives exactly the same support, but it does not support Putin. There will only be a marginal impact on aid to Putin if you lower the tax and place money directly in your wallet instead of lowering the tax on those who choose to buy Putin's oil. We can lower the food stuff. We see that inflation is already driving up the price of food and that we have a shortage of inputs that contribute to higher food prices. Food is something everyone needs, whether you choose to buy Putin's oil or not. We can work on one-off payments to people who really need it. All the things that I have now listed are things that the Green Party has come up with in large support packages. These are things that do not help either to fuel the climate crisis or to fatten Putin's war fund. In other words, it is possible to do something in this direction without acting in the way that destroys the possibility of stopping the war in Ukraine. I ask you to accept reservation 1.

Madam President, I would like The Government has been commissioned by the Riksdag to work by all means to reduce today's extremely high fuel prices. It is about saving companies that rely on fuel for their business. It is about supporting individuals who depend on the car, so that they can actually afford to use it. In this supplementary amending budget, the government has come back with a proposal to reduce the tax to the minimum permitted by the EU. In practice, it is a reduction of SEK 1 and SEK 5 at pump from 1 May to 30 September. We believe that the price is still far too high. Reducing tax and VAT on fuel would be desirable on a permanent basis, given how taxes have increased over time. But it is uncertain whether it is a way forward, because Sweden does not have the right to decide over its own taxes. Precisely fuel taxes are governed by the EU Energy Taxation Directive. The Swedish Democrats have long pushed the issue of lower fuel prices. What we can influence is the involvement of biofuels in the Swedish fuel mix, i.e. the reduction obligation. This issue is decided at national level. The reduction obligation for diesel in Sweden is currently at just over 30 per cent. We want a lower reduction requirement – 5% for diesel and 2% for petrol. This would allow us to continue to use domestically produced biofuels with a high environmental performance while at the same time refraining from less sustainable imported alternatives that are at best only ineffective but at worst counterproductive. It should also be borne in mind that fuel consumption is increasing significantly with the incorporation of biofuels, which also increases costs for consumers. Madam President, I would like Another inconvenient fact is that after decades of efforts Sweden does not have a large domestic production of biofuels. On the contrary, demand is met with extensive imports. In the Swedish Energy Agency's report Fuel 2020, 93 percent of the HVO and FAME used in Sweden in 2020 are estimated to be imported. Due to the requirement for high mixing, Sweden's fuel operators are vacuuming the market at HVO made from slaughterhouse waste. This leads to a remarkably high demand for HVO from large-scale slaughterhouses. A discussion on the sustainability of consuming slaughterhouse waste from Australia for transport in Sweden, for example, has unfortunately not been held. Neither the unethical aspect of favouring large-scale slaughterhouses that do not live up to Swedish animal welfare at slaughter nor the long transports over the earth is mentioned. These transports are not carried out using sustainable fuels. The European Commission recently called on Member States to reduce the involvement of biofuels due to the high food prices in the Union. We should therefore focus on using our arable land for edible crops for safety reasons and for the risk of lower access to food. Madam President, I would like The Swedish Democrats believe that fuel policy needs to relate to reality and stop working towards both households and companies. The criticism that lower fuel prices would lead to increased emissions simply does not hold up. The price at the pump would also be extremely high with our proposal for several crowns reduction by severely reduced reduction obligation and hardly pose any risk of us enjoying driving our cars. Finally, I would like to support the proposal in the report.

They may even be able to win in this struggle for the freedom of all of us, if they only get a little bigger gutter of support militarily and humanitarian from us in the free world. They help thousands of Ukrainians to start learning Swedish in the country they now temporarily call their home, and they need more resources because the money is over. For all these reasons, we have put forward a request for an addition to this amending budget, namely to vigorously increase resources for Swedish civil society. The SEK 100 million put forward by the government in the spring change budget will not be enough. There's more to it. But the war has also led to an ever worse crisis for Sweden's farmers. The farmers' costs have been extreme for a long time. As early as last year, the Center Party demanded an emergency billion to save Swedish food production. At the beginning of the year, when prices continued to rush, we thanked the Swedish parliament, the other parties, for supporting us in setting up an emergency package, which is now being implemented by the government in the spring change budget, of 1.1 billion to Sweden's farmers, through the removal of all taxes on diesel for one year from July this year and a first aid to our hard-tested farmers in particular, but also other vulnerable industries. With the war, we have had a new emergency situation, Madam President, where prices have rushed. It's a quadruple bang for Sweden's animal friends. There are extreme prices for fuel and electricity, as well as for feed and manure. That's where the price has doubled. Swedish producers are being knocked out. Lifeworks are about to be crushed. Swedish food production is threatening to be destroyed. Pigmeat production may collapse by more than a quarter. This is happening precisely as our total defence requires that Swedish self-sufficiency of food increases from today's totally unsustainable 50%. That is why we have tabled a proposal, which has now become a very broad committee initiative, for a full retroactive abolition of the tax on diesel from 1 January 2022 and a very comprehensive package to save Swedish livestock production. We are doing everything within today's European rules, that is, what has changed and what has existed since before. We wanted EUR 1.5 billion in support under those rules, but we have also called for the government at European level to operate and create support for a block exemption for livestock production so that we can provide an equal additional amount of support. In addition, we demand that the Norrland aid – especially for farmers with long distances – increase the maximum. Here I would particularly like to thank the Moderates for their good cooperation and for putting all their strength behind this initiative. The objective of such an initiative is, of course, for Sweden's farmers to deliver in reality, as soon as possible, in order to alleviate the acute crisis as close as possible here and now as possible. That is why I am grateful for the fact that, on Monday, the Centre Party and the Government were able to deliver in cooperation the retroactive removal of the tax all the way back to 1 January and all the efforts that are possible under the old and recently amended rules of the European Union, so that the sum reaches up to 1.6 billion. In addition, Norrland's support increased by SEK 50 million to SEK 461 million – a little more than we hoped and believed in. In total, it is now an aid of SEK 3.2 billion. For all parties working for Sweden's farmers here and now, this type of delivery is in reality what you work and work for, and you are happy when you finally reach and take a big step along the way. But that is not enough. Those of us who support the initiative also want to get the block exemption at European level. Russia's invasion of Ukraine is equated with a natural disaster for the countryside and for Sweden's animal-owners. That is why we need to maximise all the other ways we can relative to the European Union, and the government should do so. I would therefore like once again to thank the Moderates for their good cooperation in continuing to keep up the pressure on these issues for Sweden's farmers. This budget also proposes a temporary reduction from 1 May in the tax on petrol and diesel down to the EU's minimum levels of taxation, which we have also called for – not least in order for many people in the Swedish countryside to cope with the fuel shock. Then we would also like the tax on all biofuels, including those mixed in with diesel and petrol, to be permanently removed for the sake of the climate and the countryside. It is a necessary temporary step. Unfortunately, these are busy, even evil, times for all those who fight in Ukraine, for all those who flee Ukraine, for all those who help the refugees at home with us, for all those who suffer the economic consequences of war, not least for Swedish countryside and our farmers who are so central to healthy and safe food – for our self-sufficiency and our independence in Sweden. I am proud that we, Madam President, together with others, can make a difference to them in this House.
i-K9AmG6bJgjYDL4uHc4DuNh	Madam President, I would like The report before us deals with what, as far as I can judge, is the eighth supplementary amending budget which the government has submitted to Parliament this year, and two more points on which the Committee on Finance is proposing announcements. In the supplementary amending budget, the main issue is a proposal for a temporary reduction in taxation on petrol and diesel down to the EU minimum level. Parliament has pushed for such a reduction. The Left Party agreed with the Moderates on such a proposal to the Finance Committee. As I have said before in this speaker's chair, we have been prepared in the past to take initiatives and, if necessary, to make arrangements in Parliament's committees together with most other parties on specific issues where it is possible to find good solutions and the lowest common denominator. This is such a situation. From the perspective of the Left Party, it is important that the public can take responsibility for societal problems and challenges where individual households and companies have very different conditions to cope with sudden and unexpected events that they have been unable to influence. Strong and rapid increases in the price of fuel, including as a result of Russia's invasion of Ukraine, constitute such a situation. It is something that strikes very different depending on who has the ability to quickly change their usage pattern, and it is very different what its consequences are and what the conditions are for dealing with those consequences in a reasonable way. Families who depend on the car have major financial problems when prices run. For many businesses, costs are high, and there is a risk of job losses. The Left Party believes that taxation on fuels is an important instrument of climate change change and will continue to be so. However, it is not a reasonable view that the price of petrol and diesel should be as high as possible as quickly as possible. Instead, a balance always needs to be struck so that we steer away from fossil dependence while public transport and other alternative forms of transport can be expanded, so that people have time to change and so that their economy does not suffer too much from sudden price rises. This can be the case with an effective policy of conversion. It requires systematic and structured work in a number of areas and, at the same time, readiness to act to deal with short-term shocks and setbacks. What we have today is not a debate on the overall direction of economic policy. We had one, for example, when the government's economic spring bill was presented last week. We'll have more of those. I would just like to emphasise that an economic policy that does not focus on climate change cannot be classified as a responsible economic policy. If this policy is to go beyond declarations and individual signalling, it must be aimed at redeploying large parts of our society, making investments that count in hundreds of billions, taking institutional approaches that are much more robust than different types of benchmarking and guilty statements in different contexts. That is the political orientation that we in the Left Party are taking forward our economic policy, and I believe that it is becoming increasingly clear to more and more people that it is necessary to turn the debate on economic policy in such a direction. With regard to the proposal for a temporary reduction in the tax on petrol and diesel included in this amending budget, I note, like the majority of the committee, that this involves dealing with one of the four points included in the Parliament's announcement. There are three further points that are about working towards an exemption in the EU that allows for an extended temporary reduction, to temporarily review the design of the reduction obligation and to come back with a more forward-looking model to deal with price shocks of the kind we have seen now. We expect the government to continue to work there, and that is something we will be monitoring. What I note with some sadness, quite frankly bordering on dismay, is that we could also have had a supplementary announcement in this report calling for additional funds for public transport. For us in the Left, it has been important that the temporary reduction in fuel prices can be complemented by other measures. We were therefore pleased that we were able to agree with the Moderates and Christian Democrats on an initiative for an announcement calling for 2.5 billion in addition to public transport. We know from both the general debate and the warning signals in the regions and from contacts with the parties concerned that there are very large deficits that the regional public transport authorities have had to bear, not least in the wake of the pandemic. Although much of public transport goes on electricity, it is also affected by increases in fuel prices to a slightly different degree in different parts of the country. This means that there are significant risks of increased prices and service in public transport. Then it is particularly regrettable that the Center Party and the Green Party, which initially signalled that they also saw the need for additions to public transport, turned and joined the government and the Swedish Democrats on this issue. Of course, you can change your mind if there is new information or if you get some kind of inner awakening. In this case, however, it has a rather strange effect in the public sphere, where it has appeared to many people that the said parties have agreed that public transport needs more support and supported an initiative in order for that support to be provided. Even in the regions, there have certainly been those who have expected this support to come in this year and who are now experiencing something of disappointment and confusion. It is strange that it has become this way, not least on the part of the Green Party. The arguments put forward are, firstly, that this is the responsibility of the regions and, secondly, that it can stand up to the ordinary budgetary process. It is only to be noted that it is not arguments that stand out very well if it is considered that public transport is important for people's everyday lives and for climate change. It is clear that it is the fundamental responsibility of the regions, but it is also clear that there are a number of areas in which the state has seen it as important, in the extraordinary circumstances of recent years, to provide funds. It is about balances, and I regret the balance that the government, the Centre Party and the Green Party have struck here. To say that the issue should be included in the ordinary budgetary process postpones the treatment until after the elections and ignores the processes that are going on in the regions. Without this signal and these funds from the state, however you twist and reverse it, there is a significant risk of less accessibility in public transport in Sweden, which will also be a new starting point for development in the future. It is simply not a good balance in a situation where the needs are obvious and it has been perfectly possible to provide funds through additional amending budgets to a number of other activities that have been under pressure. I think it is important to say that this says something about the discrepancy between image and pose on the one hand and actual action on these issues on the other, but also about which groups can make their voices best heard through representatives in parliament and government. This makes it all the more important to continue pushing for reforms, both short-term and long-term, which can, on the one hand, provide support to people with small margins when the cost of living increases and, on the other hand, can support a climate change shift based on the differences in resources and opportunities that exist in our society today. Finally, Madam President, I would just like to emphasise that the Left Party is behind the announcements on support for civil society and the proposal for authorisation to enable surplus doses of vaccine against covid-19 to be donated to other states, to the global vaccine procurement mechanism Covax or to other international organisations. It is something that we have called for in the past, and it is good that it can now become the decision of the Riksdag.

We are therefore very proud and pleased that we have succeeded first with support package 1 and now with a second package together with the government and many other parties that have fought for this. That is, in fact, absolutely necessary at this point in time. I myself am an old farmer, and I know that the anxiety among the peasants has probably never been greater than right now. There is a greater concern today than it was in 2018. This is largely due to the post-crisis crisis, which means that the money is in practice out of the account. You can see the effect very clearly. For example, fertiliser prices have gone from the normal situation of SEK 2.50 to SEK 3 per kilo to up to SEK 12 per kilo, i.e. a quadrupling of the price. This causes all calculations to fail. The biggest effect of the war is that feed prices have risen. The cereal price, which is totally governing because if we can have animal production, is now over $1 (U.S.). This means that all old calculations for all animal production fall. This is very far-reaching, and that is why we, together with the Moderates, have supported what I would call a peasant package 3, namely to demand a block exemption in the European Union in order to move forward. I know it is not an easy road, but it is a very reasonable way to walk, given that the war situation actually makes this much worse than a drought situation. In the long term, the Center Party wants to move on to reclassifying agriculture from today's classification as an environmentally dangerous activity to a socially beneficial activity – for this does not last. We also see very clearly another thing that needs to happen, outside and partly outside political influence. For example, when an animal farmer today has to have 1 crown more per kilogram for the meat, this means a price change of 5 crowns for the consumer. 20% of the price increase made to consumers ends up in the farmer's pocket, while 80% end up in commerce. The percentage switch depends on the old rule of thumb, but there is like no rhyme and reason in it. We can't go on like this. Although it is not more goods to be transported, trade takes 80% of the price increase. That's completely unreasonable. With this, we in the Center Party are very proud and satisfied with the support packages 1 and 2 that have landed, and we will continue to fight on that line.

i-8FF36qCQhpgZbjtZ1jBsW5	Madam President, I would like The government has finally come up with a bill containing a special crime for the repression of honour. It proposes the introduction of a new offence in the Code of Criminal Procedure with the designation of "honorable repression". As a consequence of decades of irresponsible migration policy combined with a demandless and lax integration policy, honorary repression has established itself as a serious social problem in Sweden. The Swedish democrats have warned against this development. Unfortunately, however, action did not exist until the phenomenon has become an established fact. Violence, threats and oppression in the name of so-called honour are evident in most of the cultures from which we in Sweden have received large numbers of people without making demands for adaptation to our culture. The repression as such may consist of already criminal acts, such as assault and threats of violence, but it may also involve more severe criminal acts, such as implied threats or more systematic harassment. Since these acts are often carried out by close relatives of the victim and are carried out by mutual agreement within the family and family or among other perpetrators, it has been difficult for these victims to obtain redress. The Government's proposal is therefore a step in the right direction – although it should be pointed out that it is the result of Parliament's work in this area. We have been dragging the government behind us, for example through announcements. Since before, we have been given a special penalty base, and now the government is proposing a new penalty which punishes repeated honour-related violence and repression against persons. This is good, but the Swedish Democrats find that there is room for improvement. Honourable oppression, as I said, often occurs in a collective of people where some may be more pro-motivated and stand for hatred and threats while others, through their passivity, allow it to happen. They may even actively enable oppression by, for example, keeping an eye on the victim for someone else in the family or communicating information to the actual perpetrators. The government has dealt with the issue of their responsibility by advocating that it is sufficient for a participating person to have an attitude of indifference while the offender has qualified intent. In other words, the proposal does not propose the introduction of special rules for participation in the penalty clause on the repression of honour. This we believe may need to be corrected afterwards, when we know how the purpose assessment has been carried out in the courts and if the purpose of the proposal is fully achieved. We therefore believe that the government needs to follow up on how well involved are being prosecuted in order to possibly supplement the law with special provisions for participation in the future. According to us, the Government will therefore commission the Crime Prevention Council to review the implementation and effectiveness of the proposed law. In addition, we see a need for a review of the notification obligation. There is already a notification requirement for healthcare and school professionals and others who come into contact with children and young people. These notifications are submitted to the Social Welfare Board, which then opens a case and may carry out an investigation. However, whether these are adequately investigated or reported to the police is somewhat unclear, why in our follow-up motion to the bill and subsequently in one of our reservations we propose a review of that very thing. Madam President, I would like The Swedish Democrats also believe that it is morally difficult to stand free from guilt in a family where honorary oppression is practised even if you do not actively participate in it yourself. Therefore, there is reason to consider strict liability for a certain circle of related parties. As in the case of child marriage offences, where parents or other persons controlling the child can be held liable if they allow the child marriage to take place, persons in a family member's group with control over the child should also not be able to stand without responsibility if they passively allowed honorary pressure to be exercised by others. Such a regime needs to be carefully investigated as to which relatives should be held responsible for the repression and what kind of inaction should be considered to be reprehensible for criminal liability. In the light of these considerations, I would like to endorse reservation 6 on strict liability for relatives.

i-8aXkmNxBBSq7oPxy4bDstZ	Madam President, I would like We are dealing with the Committee on Transport's report, which also includes the Government's proposal to implement amendments to the directive on environmental requirements for the procurement of cars and certain public transport services. In this case, I would like to say that the committee is in fact fully in agreement with the constitutional changes required for the implementation of the European Parliament and Council directive on amendments aimed at promoting clean and energy efficient road transport vehicles. This means that Member States should be able to ensure that contracts awarded following the procurement of vehicles and certain services, including public transport, meet the requirements of a minimum proportion of environmentally friendly vehicles, so-called minimum targets. The committee shares the view of the Center Party and the Government that consideration should be given to the actual climate impact of a fuel from a life cycle perspective and that sustainable biofuels are a climate-friendly option in many procurement situations. Only a few weeks ago in committee, when we examined the report of the Committee on Transport and Tourism, we stated that the regulation on environmental and road safety requirements for government cars is aimed at promoting the use of environmentally friendly and safe cars. We also pointed out that the government has announced that gas cars will continue to be classified as environmental cars by state authorities. The committee has not changed its mind here in any way. Especially against this background, we find that there is no basis for taking any action in response to the motion request of the Center Party. This legislative proposal is the implementation of an EU directive. There is no possibility for us in Sweden to change the definitions contained in the directive, but this is regulated in the EU's own directive. This means, for example, that a clean light car may emit a maximum of 50 grams of CO2 per kilometre and that 38.5% of the total public procurement must meet this limit value. The requirements for clean vehicles, as defined here, therefore apply only to a small part of the total procurement of vehicles and transport services. The committee notes that it will still be possible to procure both gas-powered light vehicles and buses using HVO 100. Therefore, Madam President, I would like to support the proposal in the committee's report TU14 and therefore also support the Government's proposal to amend the directive on environmental requirements for the procurement of cars and certain public transport services.

Madam President, I would like Today's rules of representation are widely criticised. An investigation has made suggestions for improvements, but we do not yet see any government initiatives to address the problems. The rules have a bankruptcy effect. An entity that is in a temporary liquidity crisis is automatically subject to representative liability. In order to avoid personal liability, the Board of Directors must request the company's bankruptcy. This means that many companies that may temporarily not have the means to pay their debts are called for bankruptcy in order for the board to avoid personal liability. Mr President, I would like to Iran is undoubtedly one of the hardest and most authoritarian dictatorships of our time. Human rights violations are numerous and widespread. Despite authoritarian rule, which does not allow opposition, people go out in protest against the regime. Thousands of people have been subjected to arbitrary arrests and excessive and deadly violence during demonstrations in the last three years. The presidential elections in June 2021 saw Ebrahim Raisi as the winner, with a turnout that was the lowest ever, 48 per cent. In Tehran, the capital, only 26 percent participated. The choice was a facade. There were 600 candidates who were disqualified from standing up, including many of the reformists. The new President Raisi stands for an even more hard policy, if that's even possible. He himself is subject to sanctions from the United States after being responsible for extrajudicial executions in 1988 and led the great, nationwide crackdown on the protests in 2019. Human rights violations in Iran are at risk of worsening even more. The Left Party will continue to pay attention to the situation in the country. Sweden can do more to support the fight for a free and democratic Iran, and Sweden, as an individual country and as a member of the EU and the UN, can consistently pay attention to the gross and extensive violations of human rights in Iran. Women are in a particularly vulnerable situation in Iran. Men's violence against women is widespread, and criminal proceedings are almost non-existent. In Iran, legislation is explicitly discriminatory against women. A married woman cannot obtain a passport or leave the country without her husband's written permission. According to Iranian civil law, a man also has the right to decide over his wife's profession. A woman's testimony in court is worth only half of a man's, and mothers lose custody of their children at a very young age. In the country, it is also criminal to have sex outside of marriage. Women who have been raped are therefore in danger of being punished if they are not believed. This is also a major obstacle for women to even report sexual abuse or seek care and help. Moreover, rape in the law is defined only as forced sex outside of marriage, which means that rape within the marriage is not considered rape. Nor is there a ban on domestic violence by men against women. In this context, the courageous human rights activists within women's organisations must be highlighted, who are fighting daily for women's and girls' rights in Iran. In the last two years, peaceful demonstrations have increased, while more women's rights activists have been prosecuted on a gender-discriminatory basis. We must give all the support we can to them. Iran's ethnic minorities, such as Kurds, Azeri, Arabs and Balucher, are subject to widespread discrimination, and their ability to express their language, culture and identity is clearly limited. The demands of ethnic minorities for respect for their rights are met with widespread persecution, imprisonment and, in many cases, the death penalty. Freedom of religion is extremely limited in Iran. Sunni Muslims, Bahai and other religious minorities face great opposition from the regime in their attempts to express their faith. One of Iran's largest ethnic minorities is the Kurds, which make up about 10% of the population. Many Kurds experience discrimination in the labour market, and the group is clearly under-represented in public posts. The situation of Kurdish organisations and activists is particularly difficult. Organizations are regularly banned, and activists are arrested, imprisoned and executed for crimes against what is called national security. Sweden, as an individual country and as a member of the EU and the UN, should pay attention to the gross and extensive violations and violations of ethnic and religious minorities in Iran. In Iran, child labour is also a major problem, and it is increasingly reported about trafficking with children. Child marriage is common. The legal minimum age for marriage is 13 for girls and 15 for boys, but fathers can apply for permission to marry their daughters at even younger age– it can be as young as 9 years. According to Iranian law, girls are of age from 9 years of age and boys from 15 years of age. There are extensive reports of children being subjected to extrajudicial disappearances, arrests, torture and even the death penalty. Iran has been recognised in recent years, among other things, as one of the countries that execute children. Amnesty International reported in 2021 that over 80 people are waiting to have their death penalty executed for crimes they committed when they were children. Iran has ratified the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child but with the reservation that it should not conflict with national law and Islam. Ratifying the Convention under such conditions risks undermining the Convention and implying that it has no impact on legislation. Here, Sweden must stand up within the UN system in order for Iran to live up to its international commitments on children's rights. Mr President, I would like to More than a hundred years ago, my grandfather's cousin went to China, to East Turkestan, what is now called Xinjiang, in the northwest corner of China, near the border with Tajikistan and Kyrgyzstan. There he worked for a few decades to help the locals in various ways, with education, dental care, printing and other things. He was also a skilled amateur botanist and helped to document and determine the local flora. When the Communists took over the country, the family was forced to flee under great hardships. Perhaps it is because of the warmth that the old man expressed to the Uighur people and to his vivid stories about the popular life there that the Chinese regime's modern abuse of this distant Turkish community, the Uighurs, appears with such sharpness and terrible sharpness. In a systematic way, the Chinese have for decades tried to wipe out Uighur culture. Chinese residents have been rewarded for reducing the proportion of indigenous people, for example by allowing Chinese families to have more than one child during the decades of one child policy if they settled in Xinjiang. The traditional Uighur houses and cultural buildings are demolished and replaced by concrete complexes. The Uighurs are forbidden to speak their language. Some 1 million Uighurs are imprisoned in concentration camps to reprogram them. They are forced to sterilize and steal body organs from them. It is a purely bestial dehumanization of an entire ethnic group. The data on China's treatment of the Muslim minority group Uighurs require the action of the outside world. So about 1 million Uighurs are in re-education camps. This is the largest mass incarceration of an ethnorelic group since the days of World War II. According to a report by the Newlines Institute for Strategy and Policy and Raoul Wallenberg Centre for Human Rights, published in 2021, the abuse of the Uighurs meets the criteria for genocide. There we have said from the Moderates that if we have a bilateral relationship with the recipient country in question, such as aid, we should make demands on the recipient country. One such country is Iraq. But we also see that enforcement barriers arise in cases where Säpo has made it clear that the individual is a security threat and is to be expelled. There, the Swedish Government must seek more diplomatic guarantees. Of course, it should not violate legal certainty or any contractual obligation, for example in relation to the risk of torture. However, as far as possible, we must seek diplomatic guarantees from countries in our surrounding world that will bring home their citizens. We cannot have Islamists and extremists living and working in Sweden who are a security threat to our democracy, our way of life, our equal society. They should not be allowed to express opinions – and not just express opinions but act – in order for our way of life to change based on their world view. They're not democrats. In their society, there is only room for one opinion, and one must conform to them. In our society, it's different. Look at the chamber! Here there are eight parties with eight different ideologies that express what our society looks like. In here it is in the speaker's chair that we argue and try to convince each other, and when the debate is over we thank you for a pleasant debate. But in the society of extremists and Islamists there is no room for this. There are many such examples in the world around us. Just look at the Middle East, where religious fundamentalists run the country.
i-TQYJUzdgx2UVQ23imxgxNi	Madam President, I would like I am relatively new to the committee and take part in this debate for the first time. To prepare myself a little, I have tried to think about why Sweden has ended up where we have ended up, with a somewhat absurd regulatory framework that has had such serious consequences that it is hardly possible to review even during my lifetime, and I am still not that old. Those who have been responsible for this policy area, migration policy, the last few decades have fundamentally changed Sweden, and we have come to a point here and now where it can no longer be denied that this change has largely been negative. My thesis in this is that many in political Sweden experience a kind of moral discrepancy between, on the one hand, an obligation to help vulnerable people, often expressed in a reference to international asylum law or something like that, and, on the other hand, the repercussions that this has here in Sweden in the form of religious fundamentalism, stoning against police and civil protection, systematic violations of children's human rights, mass unemployment, human trafficking, assistance fraud, gang crime and so on. The effect of this discrepancy has become a regulatory framework which, on the one hand, maintains a facade of regulated immigration, as expressed, for example, in the general visa requirement. Anyone can therefore not just fly to Sweden. It is also a regulatory framework that, on the other hand, has so many and so large loopholes that in practice it is free for anyone to try to move to Sweden for any reason and be supported by the public for the rest of their lives. The key to the question of what to do in the future, Madam President, I think lies in whether this moral discrepancy is real and actually exists. First of all, the possibility of seeking asylum in Sweden does not mean that this normally requires an irregular journey with a smuggler, a particularly great help to the world's poor. It's the other way around. What Sweden and the EU are doing in practice is holding up a purse in one hand and telling poor people in unstable countries that if you empty your bank accounts, go on a deadly journey organized by the local mafia network or terrorist organization and manage to bring you here alive, well, then this money can be yours – perhaps. At the same time, we all know that large-scale asylum reception here by people from across the world is not only unnecessary but also totally unsustainable. That is why we are also holding up a purse in the other hand as payment to border guards or coastguards in North Africa and Turkey, among others, so that they can stop the same migrants with different, more or less humane methods that we are attracting here at the same time. For those who have an intact moral compass, this is, of course, not only wrong but completely absurd, and for such a person there are only two ways forward. Either we abolish the visa requirement and allow the whole world to fly here to seek asylum, which would quickly mean the end of Sweden as a country. This is something that no one except environmentalists is crazy enough to suggest. Or we are abolishing the possibility of seeking asylum here after an irregular entry, which would mean the end of this deadly cat-and-mouse game, something that no one but us is upright enough to propose. But, perhaps someone asks, the rule that everyone should have the right to enter Sweden and seek asylum here does not come from this international regulatory framework that we must relate to? This is something many often claim. The answer to that question is no. If there is a myth about migration that I would like to put down once and for all, it is precisely the idea that Sweden would have an obligation to let anyone travelling through Denmark or Germany to Sweden to just increase asylum. There are, of course, international asylum rules; it certainly does. These rules are based on the UN Convention on the Status of Refugees, but the rules do not at all concern the question of how a country should deal with a person travelling between ten different safe countries on the way to a particular country that is preferred for purely economic or other reasons. The fact that there is any obligation for Sweden to allow such persons in is also denied by the Executive Committee of the UNHCR, which interprets the Refugee Convention. For the record, I shall say that it is number 58 from 1989, paragraphs (e) and (f) essentially. In addition to these consequences, the black market created by the asylum system also finances terrorism and organised crime, which further destabilises and creates poverty, which in turn creates more people who want to seek a better life elsewhere and so on. As Sweden becomes a product sold, mafia networks often build up completely unreasonable expectations among their customers. It is not impossible that the widespread dissatisfaction with Sweden that exists among many migrant groups is due to an understanding that you have simply been cheated when you have bought this ticket. This industry also effectively disables the asylum process, as the package tours also include a history that is then repeated at the Migration Agency. We probably know almost nothing today about who actually needs protection and who does not. The selection of who receives asylum in Sweden depends much more on who can afford to buy the ticket than on who actually has protection needs. Last but not least, all of this has a very negative impact on the EU's foreign policy capacity because the rogue states that we have in our neighbourhood always have a card that they can play, namely using migrants as a means of blackmail. The recent negotiations on the so-called Turkey agreement or the migration crisis in Belarus are clear examples of this. Madam President, I would like If the moral imperative does not force us to keep this turning door open, but rather – if we are to be honest and if we understand the situation for what it is – forces us to close it, while the international regulatory framework does not directly affect the situation, how are we to act? In view of the clearly unsustainable reception we have had in the past, we suggest that Sweden's efforts be turned into a focus on help in the area and that the asylum rules be designed according to a simple test to decide whether or not a person should be received here: Is there a need to go to Sweden in front of other countries? Is there an international obligation for Sweden to accept a certain person? If the answer to these questions is no, the person should not be allowed in here. Typically, this would mean a 'no' for people from Afghanistan, Somalia, Iraq, Iran and so on who come up with spontaneous applications. This also applies, of course, to quota refugees. On the other hand, the second question is partly answered in the affirmative vis-à-vis people from Ukraine, for example, because the border with the EU is the only way out for most Ukrainians and because Ukrainians have the right to travel freely within the Union. If we are going to stick to this principle, how are we going to achieve this? The first is, of course, that we should follow Britain's example and establish proper border controls in other countries. Britain has its border controls on the French side of the canal. We could have them in Denmark, Germany and Poland at ferry terminals. All of our neighbours have been wise enough to keep volumes significantly lower than we are, so there is no reasonableness whatsoever for migrants to continue to choose to travel to Sweden. It is perfectly possible to apply for asylum in Denmark, which is a safe and pleasant country. The second thing we should do is to follow the Danish and UK efforts to relocate the entire asylum process to safe third countries so that people who nevertheless manage to enter irregularly can have their case tried according to laws and regulations, just not right here. The third thing to do is to adapt our asylum and family immigration framework to the true minimum level of the European Union. In this respect, I would like to endorse Amendment No 2 in the report. The most remarkable statement made by the left in this parliamentary term is that, with the changes that have been made, we would be at or near the EU's so-called minimum level, and there have been tears of crocodile tears over this supposedly strict regulatory framework. It is possible to claim that we are at a European minimum if we are simply to ignore the fact that we are more generous than is required in terms of dependents' livelihoods, the right to family migration, the requirement to obtain permanent residence permits, access to welfare, access to health care, cash benefits, the possibility of living as undocumented, the possibility of procrastination by appeal, the possibility of refusing to cooperate in the asylum process without any negative consequences, the possibility of working while seeking asylum, the possibility of a change of track, access to publicly funded legal representation, access to interpreters and so on. It is also important to remember that the fact that the number of asylum seekers is relatively lower now than a few years ago is largely due to the pandemic and the external measures implemented by the European Union, such as the Turkey Agreement and the more or less professional border police that we fund in a few different countries. In addition, the number of asylum seekers is increasing quite rapidly right now after restrictions have begun to escalate, even apart from all Ukrainians who are coming. As I always stress in this regard, the problems we have in this area are not primarily due to the European Union or to some external regulatory framework that we have to deal with, but most of all to Sweden. The damage we have done to Sweden is self-inflicted. No one can claim that responsibility lies elsewhere. I see that I have oversold my speaking time a little, Madam President. I'm sorry about that.

i-RRUE6D2nFcQJ1DDf7A5LWJ	Madam President, I would like I, like Rikard Larsson, reserve the right to describe not least my own politics – I usually invest more in it than in keeping and describing other people's politics. As to why one person chooses one country over another, there is quite a lot of research. It's pretty much in agreement, even if it's sprawling-- you're supposed to be honest and say that. There are three clear reasons for choosing one country over another, and they can easily be summarised as follows. The first is about the diaspora. If there are people from their own country, it increases the likelihood of choosing that country. The second reason concerns the possibility of obtaining a residence permit. We saw this when the Migration Agency introduced permanent residence permits for Syrians in 2013. Then traffic increased to Sweden but not to any other country, because there had not been this rule in place. The third reason concerns the design of welfare systems, i.e. the possibility of taking part in welfare. A Danish study from 2019, I think, called The Welfare Magnet states just that. Welfare is a magnet that attracts people to choose one country over another. But it is absolutely true that it is not possible to say absolutely what it will be like, because it is sprawling. However, if you read the research reports, you can see that it is these three reasons that are highlighted by the researchers themselves.

i-GYVW16aRdEeVSYSiT2vTo	Madam President, I would like Nearly one and a half million households in Sweden currently live in rental apartments. Madam President, I would like Today, the House is dealing with the civil committee's report No. 5 Right to rent, etc. Fortunately, there are two proposals for announcements that could represent a few steps towards a better functioning rental market. Madam President, I would like Conversion from a right-of-occupancy apartment to a right-of-occupancy apartment means an opportunity for residents to gain greater influence over their housing. It also means an opportunity to obtain relatively quickly more owned dwellings in areas that are currently dominated by rental apartments and where the market conditions for new production of residential units or owner-occupied apartments are poor. Enabling larger parts of owned housing in areas dominated by rental rights also means that people who live there do not have to move out of the area in order to start a housing career, which promotes the social composition of the local community. We Moderates are in favour of conversion from tenancy to tenant-ownership but also want it to be possible to transform into owner-occupied apartment. The form of ownership apartment, which is very common abroad and which was introduced in Sweden by the Alliance Government in 2009, differs from the right-of-occupancy apartment in that the owner owns the apartment itself as his own property, while the purchase of a right-of-occupancy apartment buys only one share in the right-of-occupancy association with the right to use a special apartment. If you own an apartment, you are a member of a common-law association instead of a right-of-occupancy association, as in the case of right-of-occupancy housing. The responsibility and influence of the Association is less than that of a right-of-occupancy association, which gives more freedom to the owner. Today it is only possible to create new owner-occupied apartments, not to transform them. However, the Swedish Parliament announced to the government already in 2016 and then again last year, 2021, that restructuring into owner-occupied apartments should be made possible. This work has still not even been properly started by the government. We believe that this should be done immediately, but also that conversion from the right-of-occupancy to the right-of-occupancy apartment should be made possible. I therefore support the committee's proposal in paragraph 9. Madam President, I would like We Moderates also want to enable and promote rental purchases. Rent purchase is a collective concept for different models that means that a household initially rents a dwelling with the aim of eventually owning it. Hire purchases are a very widespread and common type of business deal for the purchase or rental of movable property, but they are still very rare in the area of renting and selling of dwellings. There are international examples, such as the Finnish model Omaksi – excuse my Finnish. But attempts have also been made on a small scale in Sweden. Only in 2016 and then again in 2021, the Swedish parliament announced to the government that it should investigate the possibility of promoting a rental purchase system, so that more people have the opportunity to own their housing. No investigation has yet been set up. We believe that this should happen immediately. That is why I would also like to support the committee's proposal in paragraph 10. Madam President, I would like However, there are a number of moderate proposals which the committee's majority has unfortunately not supported. Linked to the owner-occupied apartment issue, we have proposals for simplified private rental. As I said, the actual apartment is owned when buying an owner's apartment. This means increased self-determination and no one else can deny renting the dwelling. In cases where a private person rents out an individual owner's apartment, it falls under the Act on the Rental of Own Homes. This means, among other things, that the landlord has the right to terminate the lease with a three-month notice period and that the extended property protection available in the course of an economic activity does not enter into force. Hire a private person out more owner-occupied apartments than that is considered to be precisely business activity, and the extended possession protection comes in. In order to stimulate the construction of owner-occupied apartments and at the same time stimulate the supply of quickly available rental apartments for shorter contracts, we suggest Moderates that a private person should be able to rent out up to three owner-occupied apartments. Madam President, I would like Of course, we Moderates support all our proposals and reservations, but in order to save the House time, I only ask for reservation 6.

Madam President, I would like On 1 January 2020, the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child became Swedish law. This means that the rights of the child should always be at the centre when making assessments in cases where children are involved. In short, Swedish courts choose to follow practices that put the landlord's economic interest above children's right to safe housing. In Sweden, therefore, the courts think that landlords' income is more important than children's rights. Practice takes precedence over the child's legal right to safe housing. And it is reservation 1 that I support, which deals with the valve in the case of polygamy. Since 1 July 2021, a general ban on the recognition of polygamy concluded under foreign law applies. The prohibition of recognition thus applies regardless of the parties' connection to Sweden when the marriage was concluded. Exemptions from the ban can only be made if there are exceptional reasons – the so-called valve. On behalf of the Green Party, we stand by the position we expressed in the legislative dossier on the new ban on the recognition of foreign polygamy, which was the subject of debate last year. We would therefore like to emphasise that we have the same basic view of polygamy as the majority behind the proposal today. Sweden should have modern legislation when it comes to the fact that we do not accept polygamy. We are an equal country, and that is why we should have a regulation that is clear about this. At the same time, a vulnerable party to a polygamous marriage needs to be guaranteed acceptable legal protection. What we and the other parties disagree on is how this protection should be designed. It is therefore a question of which technical solution should be chosen in order to achieve the same result. In that part, we note that the very limited exemption of the new regulation from the prohibition of recognition is in line with the proposal made by the investigation for exceptional reasons, that is to say, the valve. In addition, most of the referral bodies supported the proposal or, in any case, had no objection to it. We also note that the investigation considered the order advocated by the other parties, which means that certain legal effects of a marriage can be recognised for exceptional reasons. So you can recognize certain parts of a marriage, such as inheritance, rather than recognize marriage as such. So there is a technical difference in how to give legal protection to the weaker party in a polygamous marriage. However, the investigation revealed a number of objections to such a regime, including practical implementation problems and difficult to understand legislation. In the 2021 legislation on the confirmation of parenthood, the Government stated in its proposal that there should not be a possibility to digitally confirm parenthood before the child's birth. One might think that we should live our lives as we want, but in order to protect the weaker party, for predictability and to ensure that the best interests of the child are always put first, legislation is needed. We see in the 220 requests made by our colleagues in the Riksdag that these are extremely important issues. We also see that what is considered right, proper and morally correct varies according to one's own faith and one's political residence. It's natural, because when it comes to our private lives, we have very private opinions, but it's up to us in here to make decisions and make laws. Madam President, I would like A previous announcement, which is today a reservation only, concerns the transfer of custody and adoption of family homes. No one can forget, and we must never forget, the tragic fate of Esmeralda or the little heart, as we know her as. Her death became a terrible reminder that society does not always have the power to protect its weakest and most loved: our children. During the time after her death, the Riksdag addressed a number of announcements to the Government – and a number of proposals were also received from the Government – to ensure that the best interests of the child should always be put first. Many good decisions were made, and the children today have better protection than they had before. However, several questions remain, including when a custody transfer is to be considered and how we can facilitate domestic, i.e. national, adoptions. The Committee motion by the Moderate Committee in the Committee on Social Affairs highlights that we should consider a transfer of custody after two years instead of three years today. A transfer of custody gives the child confidence that it is in the family it belongs to. In the event of a decision to transfer custody, the child's connection to his/her host family shall weigh heavily. The child's age is also important; a young child connects more quickly to the family home. The child should see his host family as his home. Another issue that we believe is important for the best interests of the child in these complex and vital cases is that they should be dealt with by courts with specific competence in this area. We Moderates believe that matters relating to the transfer of custody should be examined by an administrative court rather than by a public court today. The reason for the low figure, 6 per mil, is that marriages that occur out of love very rarely occur between close relatives.
